PUBLISHER'S PREFACE.

Is tbk ritual accurate and the analyse of it fair and
IcandidP is doubtless the question which interests the
Reader most. ""

That the Soverpign Grand Lodge changed the ritual
f the Order mainly because of the extensive circula-
ion of our published ritual entitled "Odd-fellowship
UIuRtrated," is conclusively provi;d by the statements,
both verbal and written, of many members or the
Order, besides admissions in the public prints, which
Announced that the Revised Ritual would be fumiahed
to the lodges and Encampments in January, 1881.
Before that date we had propositions to'' furnish the
V ritual aa soon as it was issued. One of these prop-
iBitions was accepted with an agreement that ample
iiroof of the accuracy of the ritual should accompany
jrfbllow the copy.

The signs, grij>s, passwords, etc., which are known as
;he secret or nnwjitten work of the Order, were given
the lodges and Encampments orally, but all of the
est of the ritual was furnished them printed In what
re called "Charge Books." The three "Charge
iooks" contaiuing the ritual of the lodge and En-
mpment were furnished us, and every line of the
ttual was carefully corrected thereby.

The secret work had been taken down in short-hand.
lad even the description of the signs, grips, etc, is
I in the very words prescribed by the Soverc'cr
hand 'Mit**-


Aa to the faimesa and caador of the Ajialysis of the
ritual, each reader must jadge. It seems to ns that th«
statements made are all cooclusively proved by quota-
tions from standard and acknowledged authorities, auj
that the conclusions drawn are perfectly logical. Not
the enemies, but the friends, founders and exponents of
the Order are cited as witnesses.

This volume is on the same plan as "Freemasonry
Illustrated" and "Knight Templarism lilnstrated,"
which" are acknowledged to be the most comprehensive
and valuable norks on the subject in the market.

In the preface of "Odd-fellowship Illustrated," first
issued in 1874, we remarked that " careful observation
shows that very few honest men care to pay for being
put through ridiculous boys'-play ceremonies, even with
solemn charges sandwiched in between, especinlhj if
they know what theij are beforehand." We are most
thoroughly contirmeii in the opinion just quoted. The
ofBcial Btatiatica of the Ordur quoted on pages 25 and 36
of thia volume show that not only has the growth of
the Order been stopped, but its membership red iced;
and we think this is largely due to the wide circuUtion
of our former ritual of the Order. As the present
ritual is a great improvement on the other, in that it
contains most valuable information in reference to the
origin, history and character of the Order, we are en-
couraged to hope and pray that its circulation may be
still more potent, both in keeping men out of the lodge
and leading others, especially Christians, to see that
Odd'fellowahip is a false religion and therefore utterly
at variance with the religion of the Bible.

"The institution of Odd-fellowship originated
Manchester, England, in 1812, although isolated lod,
existed in various parts of the country for some time
prerioosly. * * * On its institution in Manchester
pBnglandJ, the main purpose of Odd-fellowship was
declared by its laws to he to render assistance to every
hrother who may apply, through sickneaa, distress or
otherwise, if he be well attached to the Queen and gov-
ernment and faithfid to the order." — Chambers' Ency~'
clopedia. Art. Odd-fellows.

Odd-fellowship was organized in North America inj
1819.

" Thos. Wildey and four others organized Washing-
ton Lodge No. 1, in Baltimore, Maryland, April 26,
1819, to work according to the usages of the London
Union order, A lodge was organized in Boston March
96, 1820, and one iu Philadelphia December 26. 1821,
both of which received grand charters from Baltimore
in June, 1&23. At the same time a grand charter wasj
granted to the Past Grands iu New York," — American:
Encyclopedia, Art. Odd-fellowship.

From the foregoing sketch of Odd-fellowship, the
facta of which have been taken from writers approved
by the highest authority of the order, we learn that the
order is of English origin. That it was formed by
humble, improvident laborers for the relief of those
whom their own prodigality combined with the
crushing burdens imposed on laborers by the aris-
tocracy of a dissipated, semi -despotic government,
left in a destitute condition. That after being
imported to America it gradually assumed as its
chief work the divine task of leading mao " to a true
appreciation of his whole duty, whether to God, him-
self, or to bis brother man." {Grosh's Manual, p. 35).
That the order puts beyond the reach of its cliaritiea,
remarkable instructions and other privileges, all who
are in apparent danger of needing charitable assistance.
That although only about sixty-two years old it has
several times altered its so-called secret ritual, and uaes
great care to prevent those which it excludes from its
temporal and financial benefita from enjoying its moral
and spiritual admonitions, lectures and advantages also.
Odd-fellowship duringthe last fifty years has become
strong numerically and financially, but if it is founded
upon the saitd, " great vrill be the fall of it."

CHAPTER II.

CHARACTER OF OIID-FELLOWSHIP.

The nmiii object of English Odd-feilowsbip seems
from its own history to have been conviviality and
fienaiml gratification.

IIh rounder in America seems to have had a little
htgla-r aim, and these convivial proclivities being ex-
tliided, his professed object was to develop the humane
traits of caring for the sick, the widow and the orphan.
While intoxicating beverages are forbidden in the
ludge-room, much to the credit of the order, Od-i-fel-
lownhip is far from being a temperance organizatiDn, or
even an aid to the temperance cause. Though wine,
beer and whiskey may uot be sen'ed in the lodge
(though a fln-nk of liquor from some intemperate mem-
ber's pocket is occasioually pu^ed round in committee-
room), at the close of each meeting it is a general cus-
tom (or members to adjourn to saloons; and Grosh's
dencritilion of an earl,v meeting of the order gives us a
fair picture of such n saloon gathering of the Odd-fel-
lows of lo-dny. He sa>'s:

"Tbo bwnnug, tbo pipe and the toast circulated
(rwly, M the song and jest excited their mirth and
Itil»rit,v, until by frequent r«|»ti(ion.calling for increased
indntgence, it is no wondt-r that the noble objects of '<
their meetiu KB were too frequently made liecondary to
mere nen.'^ual gratifications." — Gi^xk's Manual, p. 23. .

A ChristiAn g^ntlemitn of tfaia city tells as tlwfc be- i


■ OBAMAtatB. OV THE OHDEB. 3l

fore his converaioQ he attended the meetings of the
order; and his lodge, which was and is one of the aristo-
cratic lodges of Chicago, after adjournment used to go
to a saloon and there indulge in smoking, drinking,
card-playing, jesting and filthy eonversntion, the mem-
bers often entertaining each other hy rehearsing the
false stories about important lodgf work, etc., which
they were accustomed to tell their wives in order to ac-
count for not returning home until one or two o'clock
in the morning following lodge night.

The ninth section of the United States Constitution
contains the following clause: " No title of nobility
shall be granted by the United States." This clause
was not inserted without good reason, and Odd-fellow-
ship by educating half a million American voters to
use and love such titles shows itself an enemy of Amer-
ican civilization. It calls its collective bodies Cfrand
Lodge, Supreme Grand Lodge, Grand Encampment, etc.,
and the officers, even of the subordinate lodge, assume
the titles of Noble Grand, Past Grand and Vice Grand,
and in the Encampment we have a Chief Patriarch,
High Priest, etc. The ordinary history in some parts
In BO interlarded with grandiloquent titles as to nauseate
a refined and sensitive taste.

A comparison of some of the pompous assertions of
this order with certain of its laws, rules, etc., shows its
character to be hypocritical and false.

On page 99 of Grosh's Manual it is stated that " Odd-
fellowship is a miniature representation among a chosen
few of that fraternity which God has instituted among
men. Few na are those who would represent it, the
great principle is wide enough for all. On the broad
platform of brotherhood all nations, parties and sects

order. Eraternity in the family of mankind illui
in our family, the lodge and the order. As all men h
Goil for their father, all are brethren."

With the above or similar statements which sbooi
in Grosh's Manual, compare sections 195,1090,101
and 1101 of White's Digest of Laws, Decisions, etc.

These and others show that Odd-fellowship, the Q
called illustration of the fraternity of mankind, ei
eludes from its fellowship the defenseless, the poor, \
weak and afflicted:

1st. All women.

2d. All colored men, Polynesians, negroes, Chine^

ODD-FELLOWSHIP 18 A SECRET OHDER.

The work of the order is done in a guarded lodgilh
room, and no " book Odd-fellow," if an honorable m^
will enter an Odd-fellows' lodge pretending that he k

true character of the order, can innocently remuail
member; nor, having seen its anti-Christian chara
can he innocently refuse to lift a warning voice agal
the institution, notwithstanding the fact that formal
lodge brethren will denounce him and endeavor to^i

A clear-minded, wise student of the history and
character of Odd-fellowship will see that the only hope

Deism to Christianity is, first, to receive Christ as the
only Redeemer from siu of every kind and the source of
all real friendship, love anA truth. By thus letting in

ceoNe. Supreme love to God and equal love to man will
change the name and nature of this foe of the home, of
our simple and sublime American principles, and the

OPENING PRAYER.

" Thou King eternal, immortal, and invisible ! the only
wise God, our Saviour! Thou art the Sovereign of
universal nature, the only true object of our best and
holiest affections. We render Thee hearty thanks for
that Icind providence which has preserved us during the
past week, protecting ua from the perils and dangers of
thia life; and for permittiug U9 now to assemble in Thy
name for the transaction of business.

We humbly beseech Thee, our Heavenly Father, to
preside over our assembly, to breathe into our hearts
the spirit of love and of a sound mind; and may each
and all be governed by an anxious desire to advance Thy
glory and ameliorate the condition of mankind.

Let Thy blessing rest upon our order, upon all the
lodges, Grand and subordinate, belonging to our entire
family of brothers. Let Friendship, Love and Truth
j»revail, until the last tear of distress be wiped away,
and the lodge below be absolved by the glory and
grandeur of the Grand Lodge above. This we ask in
humble dependence upon, and in most solemn adoration
of thy One mysterious and glorious Name. Amen." —
Groak's Manual, p. 181,

CoTutuctor — Be serious, for our lesson is as melancholy
asit iH truthful. (_B!inda" are now oif ], Behold a rep-
resentation of the effect of death. That silent yet im-
pressive lecturer — to rice, confusion, but to virtue,
peace — is all that remains on earth of one who was
born as you were bom, who lived as you now live, who
for many days enjoyed his possp^siona, his power and
his pleasures. But now, alas! nothing is left of him
aaye that sad memorial of man's mortality," The warm
heart that throbbed for others' woes, or the cold one
which held no sympathy, has mouldered away and
joined its kindred dnst. Contemplate the scene! [A
solemn dirge may be sung or played]. Should it not
humble human pride? Shauld it not awake the soul to
a just sense of responsibility to its God — of duty to
itself? And in view of thjs, the common lot of all,
should it notenlistthetenderest of human sympathies?
My friend, tliat gloomy monitor is but ati emblem of
what you are sure to be.and what yonmay soon become.
Serioaaly meditate the solemn admonition it affords;
ponduf it well, and see that your own heart fosters not
evil — the bane of society, the fountain of all wrong, the
progenitor of crime, hatred and violence, whose eouse-
quencea mu^t continue to afflict mankind until that
period to which Hope looks forward with ardent joy.

INSTItllCTIONS TO CANDIDATE.

Noble Grand — In all lodges there ia an outer and an
inner door, and between them what is termed an ante-
room, At the outer door tliere is stationed an officer
called the Outside Guardian, and at the inner door an
officer called the Inside Guardian.

Wishing to visit a lodge which is open in the initia-
tory degree, you will announce your presence at the
outer door by giving one rap, or one pull of the bell, if
there be one; but any alarm will be attended to.

The Outside Guardian will open the wicket, and re-
quire from vrtu the Password of the current term, whieh
inUHt be given in full. If given correctly you will be
admitted to tbe ante-room.~ You will then clothe your-


ENITIATIOM.

Belf in the regalia appropriate to yoar rank and station I
in ihti lodge, which fortho pi'eecnt will be a plaiu wbitel
collar; advance to the inner door and give three raps.

The Inside Guardian will announce to the proper I
officer (either the Noble Grand or Vice Grand of tha ^
lodge, as the rules of the lodge shall have prescribed),
that there is an alarm at the door. The Inside Guard-
ian is directed by such officer to inquire the cause —
whereupon the Inside Guardian will open the wicket,
and obtain from you your name, rank, and tho number
of the lodge to which you belong.

The Inside Guardian will close the wicket and inform i
the proper officer, who, if he is satisfied, will direct the I
Inside Guardian to admit you, if correct. The Inside I
Guardian re-opens the wicket, and you must give him '
the Password of the initiatory degree. If correct the
Inside Guardian will admit you, when you will advance
to the center of the room and address the Noble Grand
with the Conntersign, who will acknowledge you as ajl
brother by giving the same Conntersign. Fou will then-
turn and address the Vice Grand with the Countersign,, I
who will acknowledge you as a brother with the samel
Countersign. Yon will then be seated.

Wishing to leave the lodge before ii is closed, you. J
will make the same Countersign to the Noble Grandl
which will be answered by hira.

It yon are visiting your own lodge when it is open In-I
the Initiatory degree and are without either the Password I
of the current term or the Password of this degree, thCi^
proper officer of the lodge (the Noble Grand) being so
informed asks the Secretary as to yonr standing in tha!
lodge. If the Secretary reports favorably, directiouaS
will bo given to admit you, but if the i-eport is onfavor^
able you are so informed by the Outside Guardian tkuam
you cannot be admitted.

other is emblematic j,! ol' the light of that truth which

is progressive in its character. You have passed its

dixcord, iitillH th» ittonn and soothes the spirit ot
liun, nnd dircctN in harmony man's united efforts to

liiiipiimHl fniternity in the family of man. Ourfors-
fuihora hiivi) wirtely luiulo this principle the comer-Btone

mot llfuln ffhlob ■ brother miirlMpiMi


tjf Odd-fellowship," Upon its solid basis the whole
superstructare has securely rested, and, as we believe, is
destined immovably to repose until time shall benomore.

From this principle we learn to regard the Great
Aathor of our existence as our Father," " in whom
atone we live and move and have our being;" to recog-
nize each other as alike the offspring of the same
Parent, as the master-piece of hia handiwork, and de-
aigaed as such to reflect iu our nature and relations the
image of Him after whose likeness man was formed.
We are, therefore, brothers; and in all our intercourse
we illustrate the truthfulness of this profession by re-
ciprocal relief and kindly offices to one another in the
day of trial. With the divisions and classifications of
human society our Order holds no fellowship. While
it inculcates a veneration for religion and subordination
to civil government and its laws, it studiously avoids all
affinity with systems of faith or sects, whether religious
or political.

In becoming an Odd-fellow no sacrifice of your opin-
ions, no change of your relations to_ the State, no
loosening of the obligations whidii as a good citizen,
you owe to the laws and institutions under which you
live, is required.

hood from the promptings of idle cariosity, be at once
undeceived. Mutual relief, it is true, is a leading office
In our affiliation. To visit the sick, relieve the dis-
tressed, to bury the dead and educate the orphan, is the
command of oar laws, and an imperative duty which

quent and almost daily ministrations, are but a tithe of
the intrinsic virtues of onr beloved order. We seek to
improve and elevate the character of man — to imbue
him with proper conceptions of his capabilities for
good; to enlighten" his mind — to enlarge the sphere of
his affections — in a word, our aim is to lead man to the

They, also, in apparently harmless and unimportant
particnlars, commence the perpetual lodge lesson of
BDhmissioQ and obedience to superiors. Thi» is calcu-
lated, by degrees, to weao the mind of the initiate from
the open and free subordination to law, governmeut,
And the religion of Christ, by a weekly uiglit-drill in
obedience to another system, unknown to the true God,

—the secret empire of " the god of this world."

Walking, as a risitor, through the Gray Nunnery at
Montreal, and a convent at Quebec, I saw beautiful
jroung females busy at their devotions in inaccessible
galleries in sight from the chapel floor. In a long hall

r corridor I saw pale, wan women in a row oa one side^
working with their needles under the eye of a lady
superior, like convicts under keepers; and, on the other
ride of the apartment, a row of ninety cots, beside each
' which. Bitting or kneeling, were extremely aged
|>eople whose lips moved in silence as they counted
their beads. The Papacy keeps its votaries submissive
[ Imstf. Odd-fellowship, like every false system of
religion, mast do the same; and these "opening ceremo-
" which cover six or seven pagesin describing them,
—though a single paragraph might answer the ends of
opening the lodge — are an initiatory drill of subjection,
copied, in some parts, nearly verbatim from the opening
of a Masonic lodge. Every such initiation is a mental
debauch, operating on mind like tricks, sorcery and
Ebrtuue telling.

the chains of siu, into the liberty of the aons of God.
And this contemptible farce is preceded by a aolenm
prayer to the god of the lodge, and closed with an ode
on friendship, love and truth. This is Odd-fellowship,
and the whole of it, the further degrees being but repe-
titions of similar doses.

There is but one possible explanation 'of the fact that
hundreds of thousands of grown, -rational men repeat
this farrago weekly, and from year to year. It is that
Satan is its god, and that he imposes these and other
pagan puerilities on man, made in the image of God
and redeemed by Christ, to avenge himself on the God
k whom he hates for casting him and his angels out of
n; to travesty the Christian religion and shut the
Bonly door of hope for man through Christ, by opening
[false doors leading to himself. To treat such a system
8 frivolous " nonsense " is to commit a dreadful rais-
^ take. If " the heavens and earth " were now in fiames
5 they one day vrill he (2 Pet. 3: 7) — if one only
B'bridge of escape, built by Christ, coold give safety to
L those fleeing from the conflagration of the world, and a
L revolted angel should set open false doors, promising to
r lead to Paradise, but actually leading to hell, "where
\ their worm dieth not and their fire is not quenched,"
I —such is the door of every secret lodge. Every pagan
\ ehrine, or temple, or man-made religion, ia such a gate-
way of perdition, no matter what the worahipB an> n

When Bubordinate lodges open in the degrees for the
purpose of balloting for or conferring the degrees, the
titles of the officers shall be the same as in the laitiatoiy
Degree; and the of&cers shall, respectively, occupy the
Bame chairs aa in the Initiatory Degree.

No officer or member can enter a lodge open and
working in the degrees, without addressing the presid-
ing officer, except the Conductor when he enters or re-
tires with a candidate; nor can any officer or member
retire without addressing the presiding officer with the
proper aign. All voting is by the voting sign, unless
otherwise provided.

Working Lodges must close in the Initiatory Degree
before opening in the higher degrees.

In voting to advance brethren to the degrees, when
the balloting takes place, the lodge must be opened in
the particular degree applied for, and when balloting,
the ballot-box shall be placed on a pedestal near the
center of the room, and the Noble Grand shall supervise
the balloting.


GBsr^LA^L RBeriATioKa. 77

The right of a lodge to grant two or more degrees to
^ brother at the same time is left for local legialation,

A lodge can confer the degrees upon a member of
another lodge, provided he presents a certihcate of his
lodge that they have been voted him and paid for, with
a request that they be conferred.

In all the degrees three rups call up Ihe lodge and
one rap seats the lodge.

The dramatic parts of the degrees should be acted
quite alowly. Proper pauses should occur between eacli
event in the drama, so that the candidate may be duly
impressed with each incident.

The work in the degrees must be done in regular
numerical sequence, closing in one degree and opening
in another, according to the number of the degrees.

Any number of candidates may receive the First
Degree at the same time, and in the Second Part, when
David and Jonathan kneel, members should be assigned,
one to each of the other candidates, who shall kneel
with him and claisp bauds in proper form and unite in
the covenant. Such members may be styled Assistant
Outside Conductors.

Upon closing a Subordinate Lodge in the Initiatorj'
Degree, the lodge must open in the Third Degree, and
in that degree the minutes must be read and acted upon;
then, after balloting in or conferring the First or Sec-
ond degrees, the lodge must re-open in and close from
the Third Degree.

part where all was oppression upon the one hnud, am
all was love and protection upon the other. Thus you
have seen the picture upon both sides. It is possible
that men should combine for the common good; lienee
we have societies communities, states and nations uuited
by a compact which protects their meiabers. But the
bond if) stronger when individuals are united by personal
contact and held together by ax)ersonaI covenant. Suck"
a covenant we have all entered into, and you have been.'
solemnly added to our fraternal union. By such a tie
has God bound himself to his creatures on the scroll of
heaven, with the rainbow as his seal. Such obligations
have come down to us from every age and country; by
these, the mystic rights of all nations have been pro-
tected, and men everywhere brought into fellowship..
By your solemn pledge you are now entitled to give and
receive the tokens of a deathless friendship." Every
Odd-fellow" is your brother, and his family the sacred
object of your fraternal care. You have become one
of a vast brotherhood" which extends to many lands,
and in their assemblies you will always be welcome.
The mysteries you have learned will insure you an hon-
orable reception in strange cities and distant states,

ANALYSIS DEGREE OF FRIENDSHIP.


loitb at Oatj Writ.

-JEuAvdiH:

Tbe^man; simple gnlU'^ of Sbakspeare'sdayannet
all dead yet. Who could ima^ne, were there not facts
to prore it, that grown men of ordinary sense and
capacity could act over, year after year, weekly, the
puerilities of this " Friendship Degree " in Odd-fellow-
thipl All of this "oBUEB," beyond initiation, which '
waa at first a drinking-bout in an ale-house, as Grosh
coofesseH (Manwil, p. 23), and which still retains a -
tinge from the completion of its origin, has bees in-
Vented and added by designing men for the purposes of '
greed or ambition. What else ever moved Schujfler
Co^ai to invriit an outside A^^rcc. for wnmeo? The J

ne which moved the hunch back politician, Richard, to 4
and quote Scripture moved Colfax. Ilichaid;J


OFFICE EEEKiyO — tlKCOUKS A l>EVII^ WORSHIPER. 89

wanted a crown, and he got it, Colfax wanted office,
which he gained by the same "old, odd ends stolen forth '
of Holy Writ," and aeemiug saintship.

This story of David and Jonathan, beautiful as a
sacred historic fact, acted over in a lodge, at night, by
a promiscuons body of men selected for their "sound
health " and capacity to pay dues, is literally " stolen "
for selfish ends, and becomes an affront and a sacrilege.
Its use in Odd-fellowship, like thatof all the "degrees,"
is that of splints to a weak back. Thousands of initi-
ates, when put through the initiation, feel as did Hon.
Samuel D. Greene, after being made an Entered Ap-
prentice, and sitting down beside the Senior Deacon, who
was also deacon in Greene's church. He turned and
pleadingly asked his Christian brother in a whisper:
" Deacon, won't you tell me if this is real Masonry, or
have they been fooling me?"

The cure for this rational, natural disgust at being
hoodwinked, handled and profaned, ia repetition. Now,
t'lfl initiate knows the fool's play as he did not at first.
Lni the first time a man imposes on me, the fault is
his; the second time, the fault is my own. The three
knocks of the entersign, the hollow rattle of the gayel
strokes, the mock-solemn wording and mouthing of the
lecture and the charge, all leading to the practical real-
ities, degree fees and obligations of concealment, en-
larged and deepened by repetition, and all canonized by
prayer and song; when all this is gone over with the
second time iu this " friendship degree," the dupe be-
comes a voluntary devil-worshiper. The sacredness of
his body gone by handling, his will habituated to yield-
ing without a reason, and he practicing religious wor-
^p in what he now knows to be a farce— a change


100 CHAKXED MES — ISm IS DlVESStTT. "

,:<i«ty cornea over him. He becomes possessed, and be
goes forward uoder an impulse like that nbicb lakes
the charmed bird of frog into the snake's month! His
virgin disgust is now gone, and he delights in the lodge
a» |>ra!ititutea delight in a brothel; and when onceSatan
lias Ihui! infected hi£ moral nature with his sorcery,
nothing bat the blood of Chribt can ever take it out of
him.

The t^ndless diversity in the forms of false worships
conceals from the worshiper the fact that a saper- .
natural power is evermore present in tbem. And the
rank and file of Masonry and Odd-fellowship are filled
op by yoang men artizaas, .-jludents and shopmen,
whoseoccupationorfrolie. orboth, prevents comparison. .
and generalization ; and hence the dupe of one lodge j
imagines that his is the only thing of its kind, little 1
dreaming that, as Grosh says, "one lodge is a moral copy .
of another." from the Pyramids and the " rite of Miz-
raim" down, to ajnggling spirit-circle or a village lodge.
So that whik- the poor deluded bouI supposes he is
favored ahove and beyond the rest of mankind he ia
only being put through bodily and mental processes
identical with the experience of the myriads of false
worshipers over tlie whole earth. ,

Thf heathen god Pruteus was said to escape detection
by changing his uhapes — now a tiger, a lion, a flame of
fire, 11 whirlwind or a rushing stream. So Satan shifts 1
tliL> furniB of his worships to hide his hateful presence, j
I he meanness of their moral nature and tlieir disastrous I
effects. It was " H shame even to speak " of the filthy 1
abominations covered by these pretentious and lofty 1
" mysteries " in the days of the Apostle Paul. They ■]
trere suppressed at Rome. As soon as Christianity !>&• ,'


KKlriUTS OF THE TUMPI.E — EnTB


BELIGIOIT, lOlfl


came popular they shifted their shapes into the knight- 1
hoods of the Temple and St. John : and, like Aaron and I
Jeroboam, addeda heathen ritual toan orthodox creed. T
They swindled, strutted, strumpeted, prayed and grew I
rich,till the maddened multitude whom they had imposed
upon and whose money they had absorbed suppressed
their lodges and executed them by hundreds in a day.
{See any authentic history of medieval Europe.)

The explanation of this rapid and fearful degenerat- I
ing process iu all false worships is that Satan is their 1
god — the deity of all the worships on earth which leave '
out Christ. And Satan is an unclean spirit, a " liar,"
" murderer," and. as Judas was after he entered him, a
" thief."

And a careful inspection, comparison and pondering J
of these dark orders will show that they never grow \
and spread rapidly till they begin to worship^that is,
till Satan enters them and receives what he asked of
Christ — WORSHIP. Masonic and Odd-fellows' lodges
were but semi -occasional drinking frolics till they ceased
to be trades-unions and set up worship. Then they
grew. And as nothing hardens like religious rites
practiced without religion, their leaders turned to stone.
Their secrets were re^'ealed and they murdered the
vealers. And though they cease to murder where they I
lose more than they gain by it, they are still nfirses of^l
Nihilism, whose creed is nothing but bloodt

Noble Grand — My brother, in the Degree of Friend-
ship you assumed an obUgation ivliich changed your
rfialions tr» a vast number of persons. You can never
furjtp t the hour when you were the object of envy and


hatred, and were cheered by a spectacle of confidence
and devotion. It was a moment of heart-felt enjoyment
when your hand was grasped in frieudahip, and a Totce
full of sympathy gave you encouri^ement. It was then
that two hearts melted into one in a solemn league and
fraternal covenant." By that engagement you were
bound to perform the offices of Brotherly Love. Heaven
has witnessed your vow, and the Common Father has
smiled on that compact. All Odd-fellows are now yonr
comrades, your advisers and yoiu" friends. A solemn
dnty has been devolved upon you, and you have been
taught how itought to be performed. The central link
in the chain of Odd-fellowship is " mutual assistance."
Fraternity, unless embodied iji acts of humanity, is but
an empty name. If a brother be naked and destitute
of daily food, and one of you say unto him, "Depart
la peace, be ye warmed and filled!" notwithstanding
ye give him not those things which are needful for the
body, what doth it profit? The answer is obvious: he
who witnesses suffering and does not hasten to relieve
it, is ignorant of the lesson that it is more blessed to
give than to receive. He is an enemy of hia race who
does not eare for its welfere, and is wedded to selfishness
and greed. Friendship leads to Brotherly Love, which

This degree is Christ's parable of the Good Samaritan,
dramiitiKed by, aad acted in, a lodge which excludes
Christ! The lecture calls it "a famous drama, which
baa received the plaudits of the world," but suppresses
the name of its Author! thus, literally,

• • • ■WBllBBihallvBryof IbeCourt of He»Tea

Why do Masonry and Odd-fellowship take their sa-
cred lessonsfrom the Christian Bible? Why not from
the Koran or the Book of Mormon? And why are
there no "degrees" of brotherly love taught by Odd-
fellows in the kingdom of Dahomey or the Cannibal
Islands? The answer is, the lodges depend on Christ
for their ideas of virtue. Christ has come, baa "ele-
T&ted " mankind by his teachings, sufferings, death, and
by sending his Holy Spirit on Christians; and these
Satanic lodges impudently put his Bible on a level with
the false revelations, which they nuver quote. And
when Christianity has civilized a land, these lodges

Lerly Love they dramatize Christ's wonderful paral
[■ while they treat its Author with silent contempt!
I lodge is thus deism and infidelity. If it were Ij
Ldeists and infidels would not love it as they r
KAnd, with an amazing e&routery which sinks I
fiehame, they claim superiority for the lodge
rwide religion, for casting out Christ, whose Scriptui

give them their ideas of virtue, and whose ctvilizatji

furnishes them members, degree fees and dues!
The idea that " Brotheriy Love" can be promoted J
. such a concern is simply preposterous.

A Colonel, now in our regular array, and who foQ^
^at the head of a regiment of volunteers in the horr
I battles of James River, joined the Odd-fellowa in QsS^
tburgh, Illinois, while he was a member of my Sem
i'Class in Knor College. He was an amiable young n
[and made a brave and capable officer. I remonstra^
l^with him and he left the lodge. He afterwards told^

ihat, after all their " brotherly love " professions, I
Euight on which he took, as he had a right by theiril

a take, his withdrawal card, the tone, looks and demeB

tor of his lodge brethren was such that he should h
^feared to be alotie with them that nijjht in thetooodst \

respected pastor of the Congregational church in Sat
I wich, Illinois, who had been both Mason and Odd-^

toY, said to me: " It is devil-worship, as you say."
"Why, brother," said I, "you never belooj
tihem, did you?"

" Yes," he replied. " when clerk in a busi

iu New York I joined both lodges; and as I look b


A PASTOb's TEBDICT — PAID-rOE BEKTIVOLENCE. 117

over the aights I spent in those lodges, and see, at thia
distance, the facea and mien of tlie members, the whole
thing eeems to me simply diabolical."

There is but one person in the Universe who can
overthrow this system of lodges. That person is the
" Stronger than the strong man armed."

The direst of all hatred is counterfeit love. It is the
best thing corrupted into the worst: corruptio opti'ml,
peasima ! It is, indeed, the kiss of Judas Iscariot. Such
is the " Brotherly Love " of Odd-fellowship.

True love is natural; that of the lodge, artificial.
Bible love "worketh no ill to his neighbor:" lodge love
worketh no good. Its very favor \s " for value received,'
and dies a certain number of weeks after its victim
stops paying dues. John Kaudolph said his philoso-
pher's stone was "Pay as you go." Grosh says that
Odd-feUowahip is " Pay iu advance " the dues of the
lodge. — Odd-fellows^ Manual, pp. 192-3.

And, curiously enough, a member in the Encamp-
ment must still keep paying in the lodge below, whether
he attends there or not. "/« fact^ good standing in the
Subordinal<: Lodge, which signifies freedom from disabil-
ity by non-payment of dues, is essential to good standing
everywhere else in the Order." — GrosKs Manual, p. 255,

To call such a thing " Friendship " is treason to hu-
man language! Paying for and entering higher degrees
does not exonerate from steady payment in the lower
degrees. So that, as the Odd-fellow goes on from one
degree to another in this temple of mock friendship, he
increases his taxes, and so he literally " drags at each
remove a lengthened chain." The love of husband, wife,
parents, children, brothers, friends, wells up in the ho-
tnau heart at Che mandate of nature. The artificial love


EBAL VS. LODGE LOVE — A COUNTBEPEIT.

of the lodge is pledged to Etrangers, asd springs from
grips, signs, oaths and obligations. It is not even that
love of bomanity which led the Roman poet to exclaim :
*" I am a man, and what concerns man concema me!'-
For Odd-fellowship excludes humanity from its portals
by denying its instructions to nineteen-twentieths ot
the race; and, like all other mystic secret orders, feeds
the religious cravings of man on the ashes and chaff of
its ritual; excites his veneration by scenic mystery and
show; pays back a fraction of its "dues" in benefits to
the sick — to rich and poor alike — provided they are not
too poor or unfortunate to be able to pay the dues, and
calls this swindling imposture " Brotheily Love!"

But all its manifold mischiefs center in and subserve
this one grand purpose. It is a counterfeit or mimicry
of the friendship of Jesua Christ. The New Testament
teaching is that God sends " the Spirit of his Son," the
Holy Ghost, into a soul, and he immediately calls God
his " Father," and Christ becomes his Elder Brother and
"Friend." — Jno. 15:15. He not only called his dis-
ciples " friends," but they are called " hia brethren,"
— Heb. 2: 17. This stupendooa endearment, essentially
sublime and magnificent in the very theory and thought
of it, is met and counterfeited by Satan in the lodge,
by putting His spirit on the members, leading them to «
worship him and calling them "brothers," and all out-
side of the lodges "Gentiles" and " profane!" Thus
the infinite and infinitely glorious friendship and
brotherhood with the eternal Qod through Jeaus Christ
is counterfeited, caricatured and counteracted by Satan
by means of hia degrees of " Friendship " and " Broth-
erly Love!"

Vice Grand — Brother, you are now tntering upon the
[aat degree of a Subordinate Lodge — that wliich crowns
the fabric; and we devote it to tlie principle of truth,"
which is appropriately iutroduci-d in this portion of onr
work; for it follows close after Love, and has an inti-
mate connection with it. It is the spontaneous result
of genuine and (constant Love. If you are faithful to
the duties enjoined in the last degree, Truth will appear
in all you sav and do, as an intrinsic ornament of your
character. If Love is the fundamental element of
all morality, Truth is the Imperial virtue. It ia the

THE liONDLE Op'STICKS"

No Scripture, least of all Scripture prophecy, is of
any local or particular interpretation. Rove is not
alone on the Tiber. She ia everywhere where her rites
are. For in them is " the hiding of her power." And
" the great whore, sitting on many waters," which are
the tongues, peoples and nations who have been made
drunk with her filthiness and false worships, is the
spiritual photograph of false worships to-day, as it was
when John was " in Patmos for the word of God and
the testimony of Jesus Christ."

These now, as then, "make war with the Lamb."*
Certain " United Brethren," falsely ao-ealled, denounce
their brethren who resist the moral filthiness of the
false worships, because they make their church unpop-
ular, especially in the cities, which are almost " wholly
given to idolatry," as in the days of Paul.

But if the world hateth them, we know that it hated
Christ before it hated them, "If," says Christ, "ye
were of the world, the world would love his own; but
because ye are not of the world, but I have chosen you
out of the world, therefore the world hateth you." —
John 15: 18, 19.

The world does not hate Bishops Gloshrenner and
Weaver, and Agent Shuey, and their helpers in the
church of the United Brethren in Christ. But it hates
all those who, by prayer and testimony, are seeking the
overthrow of the lodge. Though if Otterhein, and


<


148 THE 8CART.KT VEABT OF TffK APOOALTPSB.
Boehm, and Newcomer, Guething and the sainted dead
who founded that church were now here, CTery well-
informed member of it knows they woald be among the
sternest abhorrers of the lodge. And, till lately, those
who would have stood by those founders, in the wholftB
membership of one hundred and fifty-nine thousaoc
would have been more than one hundred to one.

The coincidence in hue between the scarlet w
and her scarlets-colored beast filled with the nan
blasphemy, and the scarlet caps, robes and degree o
Odd-fellowship, and the same color also prominent i
the regalia of Rome, may be thought incidental. BaH
there is no mistaking the identity of their nature. Th(
filthiness of false worship ia the same in all three,
their moral effect is the same. The people are drs
with it.

There are now about one hundred secret order
working nightly, outside and inside of our courta, leg!
latures, schools, colleges and churches; and every such ■'
order, little or large, is an apocalyptic Beast or image
of a Beast; each one is a living embodiment of an
anti-Christian force, and each lifts the same serpent
head which appeared in Eden to estrange man from his
Maker; each swears or pledges its votaries to conceal
they know not what, and each subjugates them for life
to unseen and unknown superiors. Nor is their poison
restricted to their own membership. The outside world

These all make war upon the Lamb; but, blessed be

ia opening the Encampment in the Koyal Purple De-
gree.

I proclaim this Encampment open in the Royal Purple
Degree.

Chief Patriarch — Senior Warden, what is your duty
in the Encampment?

Senior Warden — It is my duty to perform the cere-
monies intrusted to rae, and to support the Chief Pa-
triarch and the High Priest in the discharge of their
duties.

Chief Patriarch — Junior Warden, what is your
duty?

Junior Warden — To examine the Patriarchs prior to
opening; to assist the Chief Patriarch and High Priest
according to my office, and to see that the signs are
given correctly.

Chief Patriarch — Scribe, explain your duty.

Scribe — It is my duty to record all proceedings of
this Encampment [tchen the duties are divided between
a Recording and a Financial Scribe, the answer </ the
Becording Scribe ends here, ami the Financial Scribe


girta the remainder]: to keep aecarmte aceoonts between
tbia Encampment and iU members; to receive all ite
raoueysi" and par them to the Treasurer, taking hia
reteipt for the same.

Chief Patriarch — Oateide Sentinel, explain yoor
daty.

Outside Sentinel — It is my duty to take chai^ of tlie
ante-room, and to admit no Patriarch [^except a Patri-
arch with a carif} withont the Chief Password of the
term, unless directed by the Chief Patriarch so to do.

Chief Patriarch — Inside Sentinel, explain your doty.

Inside Sentinel — It is my duty to prove every Patri-
arch before he is admitted; to report his name to thf
Chief Patriarch, upon whose direction to obtain tho j
posHWurd of tbe dejrree in which the encampment i
opened, and, when the Encampment is open in tli^l
Hoyal Purple Degree, to suffer none to retire withoid
tbe password of the evening, or to return without tbi
password or the password of the degree.


Chief Patriarch—iJLost Excellent High Priest, e
plain the duties of your office.

High Priest — It is ray duty to counsel the membt
of the encampment to improve themselves in the I
tiires and Charges, as well as in the practice of t
doctrines; to conform to the General Regulations,]
thus preserve the uniform mode of working in this I


OPBKINQ CBBSHOHiaS.
mmpment; and to give such iustructiona to newly
litiated members as the good of the frateraity may
require.

Chief Patriarch — Patriarchs, my oiBce gives me
charge of this Encampment, and requires rae to see that
the respective ofEcers and brethren perform their duties
[n a proper manner. It is unnecessary now to recapit-
ulate tho^ duties, or to enlarge upon the relations which
we all bear to each other. Our own experience has
established the value of our laws and regulations, and
are pledged strenuously to support them, and be
teady at all times to aid in their execution. We are not
to palliate or aggravate the offenses of our brethren,
but in the defiision of every trespass we should jadge
with candor, admonish with friendship, and reprehend
iVrith justice. Such is the nature of the engagements
which we are bound by the most sacred ties to perform.

Chief Patriarch — Officers and Patriarchs, be attentive
while the Most Excellent High Priest offers a prayer,

PRAYER BY THE HIOH PRIEST.

Heavenly Father, we pray thee to look with favor
npon this meeting. Guide us in our efforts to improve
the time before ua; let thy care and the good we receive
at thy hands instruct us in our duties toward each
other; and so direct all our acts that thy name may be
minified now and forever. Ameu.

OBirEKAl. KEQULATIOKS.

When a brother is initiated into the Eucampment it
shalt he called admitted.

When the Golden Rule Degree is conferred, it shall
be called advanced.

When the Royal Purple Degree is conferred, it shall
be called exalted; to-wit:

Admitted to the Patriarchal Degree.

AdTanced to the Golden Rule Degree.

Exalted to the Royal Purple Degree.

MlSCELLASEOUa.

The Check Password is determined upon by the Grand
Patriarch of the State, District or Territorial Grand
Encampment. It is communicated to the Chief Patri-
arch and Senior Warden of each Subordinate Encamp-
ment at the installation of its officers, each term. After
hia installation the Chief Patriarch shall himself, or by
the Junior W'arden or other member of the Encamp-
ment, give the Check Password, privately, to each mem-
ber of the Encampment who shall he present and
entitled to receive it.

The Check Password shall be given to proper officers
of Encampments immediately subordinate to the Sov-
ereign Grand Lodge of the Independent Order of Odd-
fellows, by the District Deputy Grand Sireof the juris-
diction.

The Chief Patriarch, or in his absence the Senior
Warden, can alone give (or cause to be given) the Check
Password to members entitled thereto.

No officer, nor, of course, member, can enter or retire
from an Encampment when open and working as such,
without the proper sign, etc, (addressing the chairs),
' the Junior Warden in charge of a candidate.


15S ENCAUFUENT DEGBEGS.

The law in rfg;ii-(l to the Honors of (he Order refen '
to Subordinate ijiQcampments as well as to Subordinata
Lodgea.

The Holy Bible" ia an integral part of Odd-fellow-
shjp, and it is necessary that it should he present ia
every Encampment while open for biiainess.

qualifications' °° FOR MEMBERSHIP.

Brothers in good standing in lodges under the juris-
diction of the Sovereign Grand Lodge of the Independ-
ent Order of Odd-fellowa, and who have attained the
Degree of Truth, may be admitted to and retain mem-
bership in an Encampment of Patriarchs, subject to thft i
regulations applicable to this branch of the Order.

The High Priest of an Encampment shall, when con-
ferring any of tlie degrees, wear a robe'"' made of royal (
purple fabric, trimmed with ermine or white fabric, with
a white surplice of muslin, linen or silk, together wiUi
a mitre and breastplate.

PEBPAHATION OF CANniDATB.

The Junior Warden proceeds to the ante-room i
dresses the candidate as himself, in a i
brown gown, with a rope around the waist for a girdle

blindfolded and sometimes has a sacic ou his back a
sandats upon his feet. There is a dim light in the I
campmeni room. A curtain or screen is so arranged a
tn conceal the altar. The Watches act aa " Wildemw
Sentinels," and also as Torch Bearers. All rough usaj

INSTEnOTIOMa TO OANDIDATB.


Chiif Patriarch (to candidate) — Deiiroui of being
admitted to an Encampment, you will giv9 at the outer
door the lame alarm as in the Subordinate Lodge. The


Ontside Sentinel will open the wicket, and require of
you the Check Password of the term, tvhich is given in
full. If correct, he will admit you to the ante-roora.
Tou will then clothe yourself in regalia appropriate to
your rank and station, and, after ascertaining from the
Outaide Sentinel that the Encampoieut ia open in the
Patriarchal Degree, you will give three rapa at the inner
door, when the Inside Sentinel will open the wicket,
and obtain from you your name, rank, and the number
of the Encampment to which you belong, which he
will report to the Chief Patriarch, who will instruct
him to admit you if correct. The wicket is again
opened, when you will be required to give the Password
of the Patriarchal Degree (divided). If correct, you
will be admitted, and will advance to the centre of the
room and addresa the Chief Patriarch with the Check
Sign. The Chief Patriarch will acknowledge you aa a
Patriarch with the answer to the Check Sign. You
will then turn and address the Senior Warden with the
Check Sign, who will also acknowledge you as a, Patri-
arch with the answer to the Check Sign. You will again
turn to the Chief Patriarch and address him with the
Sign of the Patriarchal Degree. The Chief Patriarch
will give the proper answer to the sign. On leaving
the Encampment, before it ia closed, you will address
the Chief Patriarch only, with the Sign ot the Patri-
archal Degree, and that officer will answer you with the
appropriate sign.

Ch4ff Patriarch — My brother, I now present to you
black apron. It indicates that you have served as


TTR patriaechal deorbe.

II novitiate Palriarcii. You have sought employmenl
to qualify yourself to assume the high duties of man-
hood. You have traveled across the wilderneaa to reach
the Chief Patriarch's tent, and, on your arrival, a wel-
come was given you. Yon have been obligated, that
you mi(tht become a shepherd in the fieldn, on the
mountains and in the valleys, preparatory to full
recognition as a Patriarch. You aasiated in the ex-
pmplification of Abraham's faith in God. You have
I oiled ardently in the pursuit of truth, a cardinal virtue
of Odd-fellowship, and you have been instructed in the
unerring law by which you are to be governed. Vihtob
should be the great aim of your life — that virtue which
gives whatever is great and good in man. Honor,
Fidelity, Friendship, social and domestic happiness, are
but empty sounds in the mouth of any but a virtuous
man. Virtue gives joy which vice with all herflatter-
ing promises never pretends to offer, and bestows a zest
upon those joys that are common to all which they
can never have without it. Its influence spreads through
life, diverges in every direction, and penetrates every
condition. It is the guardian of youth, the honor of
manhood, the crown of age, the shield of prosperity,
the prop of affliction, our guide in active life, and our
solace in retirement. It holds the keys of life, and will
finally open to us the gates of immortality. My
brother, it is proper that you be prepared to test the
presence of an intruder, in case he should invade our
camp.

How shall I distinguish a Patriarch of this degree?

Junior Warden — By the signs and tokens of i
Patriarchal rank.

Chief Patriarch — My brother, I commend your skill,
and trust that the remembrance of these symbols and
the virtues which they imply will never fade from your
mind. Our institution is ascience of symbols, convey-
ing by striking emblems the most interesting and
exalted truths. There are momenta in the experience
of the most virtuous life when incentives are needed to
dispel the lethargy of the soul and excite it to action.

I now present to you a crook, an implement of your
vocation as a Patriarchal herdsman. It was used by
the Patriarchs of old, and strikingly reminds us of the
aimplicity of their lives and the purity of their faith.
They dwelt in tents, and, surrounded by their flocks
and herds, spent their lives in the inculcation of the
most exalted social virtues. The onward march of
civilization, and th» advance of human society, have
deprived the world of much of that simplicity and vir-
tue which gave its richest charm to the Patriarchal life.
Here, in this siiered retreat, we would revive that sim-
plicity and inculcate that purity. Here we would lay
the broad foundation of that universal brotherhood.
which shall link in the golden chain of oar ever ev

[To effect the great moral and social revolutid
individual action must be combined with oMocia
effort. Each should believe that his individual d
essential to the integrity of the whole. Ever let t
principle of generous beneficence dwell in your ha|
which, silently and unseen, like the dew of heavi
blesses and refreshes with the gentle droppings (
bounty. Cherish the beautiful teachings of our Ord
which, falling upon the heart like rays of light, j
color to every affection of our nature, and dictate e
act of our lives. Succor the stranger in distress; grp
him food and rest when he is hungry and weary, witt
out inquiring his country or creed, by whatsoei
fortune he may have fallen, by what destiny he may be
controlled. With your brother shepherd, guard against
jealousy and strife. Remember that he is your brother.
If, under the influence of imperfection, the common lot
of mortals, he wrongs you, go to him, and in the lan-
guage of affection tell him his fault between him and
you. If he repent, forgive him; enter no harsh judg-
ment against him. Conscious of your own frailty,
remi^mber that to err is human, to forgive divine.
These, my brother, are the principles we should ever
practice. A man may enter our camp and dwell beneath
our tent; mistaken confldence may invest him with our

ANALYSIS PATRIARCHAL DEGREE.

iHl«ioriiof B Fob— An KneUih Alo-lmme— TbeBlrthplaceDt Odfl-teUow

— RillEloo Ihe Cbl«t E


I The history of Odd-fellowship ia the history of a fog
'It ia a moral miasm rising from a human morass, a
its analysia is as difficult as its history. I faaye see
respectable-looking English women, wives of mechai
and laborers, sitting in rows on benches in a tap-hoiu
some with babies in their arms, waiting wishfully £c^
some one to give them a glass of beer, while, farther iaj
their husbands sat guzzling at their mugs around dir
tables in rooms filled with tobacco smoke. Such a
ale-house was the birthplace of Odd-fellowship, both j]
England and in this country.

Their standard historian, Grosh ([p. 23; see also n
22), says: "Meeting, as they did, in public houses of it
the most select character, the beer mug, the pipe s
the toast circulated freely." And on the same p«j
(23d) of bis volume Grosh gives the following chon
sang till their tongues became too heavF*


Seeing what could be made of it, cuuuiiig meD turned
this social row into a rowdy rcligioa, gave the lodge a
devil's constitution by packing it in degrees pledged to
secrecy from the world and each other — a secrecy grow-
ing dense to the top. Then they gave it a creed of one
article, which is belief in the god of this world, with
Christ left out in compliment to Jews and deists. They
named its degrees " Friendship," " Love " and " Truth,"
held up their fingers, when not too drunk, to signify
"Faith," "Hope" and "Charity;" and as more money
can be got by secret societies than by anything else ex-
cept Popery, they put on masks and red, blue, white,
purple, yellow and black robes, and Odd-fellowship
flourished I

But there came to be too many at the top of the
lodge, and an upper aide-ahow called " Encampment "
was invented, with Patriarchal, Gtolden Rule and Royal
Purple degrees. This Encampment answered a double
purpose. It brought more degree fees, and. by long
lectures which none but men of strong memories and
much leisure could commit, it increased the funds and
diminished the leaders among whom, after paying small
benefits, the money was to be, and is, divided. Thus
Odd-fellowship, like Masonry, became a model for
Mormonism.

There is a true old pagan proverb which says, " Man
is a worshiping animal." When the plough was intro-
IdmA to Asiatics, and the pedlar's back was turned, it is


ISS ASXASAM — SALTATIOX BT CSKEHOHIES.

Bftid they painted it red, set it op on end and worshiped.
it. This explains the euccess of Odd-fellowship, whea
turned from s, drinking-bout into a religion.

This First Encampment or Patriarchal Degree ifl''
saved from insipidity by theft from the Bible, like the
first two degrees of the lodge. It is to make Odd-fel-
lows Patriarchs by playing Abraham's sacrifice of Isaac,
but says nothing about Abraham's faith in the promise
of God that Christ, the Messiah, should come through
his posterity. The sublime part of Abraham's trial was
his belief that Christ should come through the posteri-
ty of Isaac, whom he was about to kill, " accounting
that God was able to raise Isaac irom the dead." — Heb.
11: 19. Of this the inventors of the Patriarchal De-
gree either knew nothing or cared nothing, or both.
They had no occasion for Christ. He is to Odd-fellow-
ship a sectary, a "stone of stumbling and rock of offense."
They were inventing a system of salvation without
Him; a system in which Odd-fellows should lift tliei
selres to heaven, as it were, by their own boot-atraps-— j
save themselves by symbols and ceremonies and


without atonement, without the Holy Spirit, the pi
chase of Christ's blood, and in contempt of that very'
Savior who was symbolized by Abraham's offering of '
son which they are playing, and the sheep which suf-
fered in his (Isaac's) stead. So they make balderdash of
the Scripture. And as the future Messiah is the
and substance of Abraham's faith and trial, this Pal
archal Degree is like the poor strollers' " play of ffai
with the part of Hamlet left out;" whili the whi

RKLIOIOS- THE CHJEF END OF THU ORDER — CHARITY. I^
aignificaiice of llie lliinir is, th.it the lodge leaders get
their money and Satan gets hia worehip,


And, be it Remembered, this extemporized, got-up
religion is, by its own authorities, declared to be the
chief end of Odd-fellowship.

An excellent little book by a Lutheran pastor — Eev.
J, H. Brockman, which every one should read, gives
abounding proof from the utterances of the Order
itself that it does not claim to be a charitable society,
but pays the weekly allowance to rich and poor alike.
Hence, as rich and idle men are quite as apt to be sick
and die as the poor and industrious, it may happen, in
a given lodge, that the poor pay in all the money and
the rich get all the benefits!

Rev. Mr. Brockman gives from Grand Lodge minutes
— year and page given — the ease of Henry L. Gunther,
wbo mailed live dollars to pay his lodge dues on the
fourth day of October. The money was received, cred-
ited, paid all his dues and left a balance in his favor.
Meantime (October eleventh) Gunther's wife died, while
the money was on the road. The letter was registered
and the money belonged to the lodge when it left Gun-
ther's hands, yet the State Grand Lodge of Pennsylvania
allowed his local lodge to keep his money, hut refused
the funeral benefit for his dead wife.

The same Grand Lodge minutes, 1871, p. 486, give
the case of Jacob Daumb, Chambersburg (Pennsyl-
vania) lodge. He sent five dollars from Ft. Wayne,
whither he had removed. The money paid up his duea


anil left a balnace of seventy-five cents in his favor.
D;iumb died while his money was on the road, and his
wife was refused the conetitutional funeral benefit.
Those men may have paid dues regularly for twenty-
five or thirty years, and drawn out nothing; but dying
five cents in debt, alter paying hundreds of dollars,
would deprive them of the funeral benefit! And as the
poor and unfortunate Odd-fellows are the ones moat
likely to fail to keep np their dues, the rich and well-
to-do members are the ones likely to receive the bene-
fits. These are but specimens of a multitude of similar
cases reported in the minutes of the lodges. For such
an order to sing in their odes of " drying the widow's
tears," etc., etc., is a mockery and profanation, as all
sensible Odd-fellows would see if Satan did not blind
their minds by his false worships.

Junior Warden (to candidate)— You see before yon
representatives of the different peoples of the earth.

There is the White Race, now scattered throughout
the world. Its origin was in ancient Asia, the region
ence occupied by Adam, Noah and Abraham, but aotv


oveiTun by semi- barbarous hordi's oi different in re-
ligiuus faith as in maimete. Many of them still cling
to the aocieot worship of JudaiBm, some to the Crescent
and some to the Cross. The religion of the Cross pre-
vails where civilization exists, and yet its devotees are
divided into sects as antagonistic in creed as though
they were dissimilar in origin.

The Brown Racb inhabits parts of southern Asia.
It is far advanced in the arts and sciences. Itstemple!<
of worship are of great splendor, and exhibit archi-
tectural skill equaling that of any other people. Ita
religion is Paganistic; and its social habits are defined
by strict and rigid caste.

The Yellow Race, consisting of the Mongolians of
Asia, are votaries of Pagan rites, according to the
teachings of Confucius. For centuries they have given
practical evidence of advanced mental power, by the
production of handiwork whose mysteries have remained
unfathomed by other races.

The Red Race consists of unlettered savages, roam-
ing through the pathless wilderness, chasing the fleet
doer with the quiver and bow. The sun, moon, and the
myriads of stare receive their profound adoration as
symbols of an eternal Great Spirit.

And there you behold the Black Race. It inhabits,
as hordes, the wilds of Africa. In general they are
barbarians and monsters in the practice of the moat
dire rapine; yet some of them are more gentle — as the
Nubians, who dwell upon the burning sands of the
Equator and dance to the music of a reed beneath (
spreading palm.

Itcbr. ariliin iDd proteiiloiml Dtairaaran ue amilted, ibe fatlowlnKlabt;


mitiatioh,

["These races are employed to some extent, as artisans,
"by whose hands are constructed palaces, temples, and
all the implements required for man's convenience and
mental culture. Some are devoted to tlie pursuit of
letters or the sciences, such as chemistry, geography
and astronomy. By their genius and mental power,
man is educated and advanced tu a higher and better
condition.]

In recognition of the Benign Power that created all
things, man seeks to honor the Creator by ceremonies
of adoration. That group embraces men of different
creeds, who are followers of the Christian religion, and
there is the Pagan, a worshiper of idols, or of the ele-
ments of fire.

And here are the intolerant and persecuting Moham-
medans, who are ever ready to immolate the man of
another creed upon the altar of their own peculiar
faith.

ANALYSIS GOLDEN RULE DEGREE.


The professed object of this degree ia to teach " the
principle of toleration." (See ritual, p. 187.) The
reai object, aa of Odd-fellowship throughout, ia to hold
up Christ's religion to contempt, by concealing ita unitj-
fmd expoding its diRsensions and divisions, and claiming
Buperiority for the motley and many-hued religiona of
Satan over that of Christ.

The object and end of Christianity as taught by
Christ himself is, " That they all may be one: aa thou,
Father, art in me, aud I in thee; that they a!so may be
one in us," — Juhn 17: 21.

To checkmate and foil this magnificently sublime
pictnre of the spiritual oneness of all God s children,
through "one Mediator" and "one Spirit," with the
inEnite God himself — and thus counteract the moral
•ffect, on the intellects of other worlds gazing on God's
redemption in this, of a cone of 8piritual oneneHa, hajed
«n regenerated souls and hiding its apex intha infimty


wfakh veiU God'a thmne. the lod^ nlisioas, yarim
M the dnat of Otwav'a " Old Wonun "* —


most needs Iiare s mock onily of their own.

And yet their amtj, like the Taonted nnity of
druskards in their cups, b all the time ending in
brawU. They are truly one only in their malic« against
Christ; and their malic« is excelled by their bald hypocri-
■y. Take this Golden Rnle Degree as a Epecimen. They
meet as a sect or lodge which exclndes more than nine-
teea-twentietha of mankind. They admit only " free
white males" over twenty-one and ander sixty, and
thoneonly when hale and able-bodied : and with tbia
badgered, blindfolded and bewildered handful of hnmai
ity they set themselves to anify mankind- They
and deplore over the sects of Cliristianitry, and tei
their own little sect, divided from other secret seel
and the ouUide world by walla of secrecy, and excludii
all who inherit the least tinge of brown or yellow
their complexion, they boast {see note 10.% p. 192,,
" Here no artificial distinctions of nation, sect; or tribtt
are known!"

To utter such lofty falsehoods must require the bra-
zen tongue and brow of the hypocrite. Yet they
insist, " The rule by which we walk is founded upon the
imniiitahle truth that all mankind are brethren." (Rit-
ual, p. ImH.) And this fearful untruth is uttered of an
order where, on the face of it, the single word "free,"
defining its membership, excludes millions of unhappy
uluven.

The degree then proceeds to act the lie which it bad
uttered. To do this they dress up some members of
their loii^e ris Turks. und when thebliiidfolded candidate

Now this play is a slander on the Turte, There are 1

The success of such swindling pretenses — so thin and I

The falsehood and frivolity of Odd- fellowship are its
protection. . Sensible people cannot believe a system so
shallow and self -contradictory can be very dangerous.
The leading Roman and Greek oracles were equally
puerile and contemptible. Cicero said he could not aee
how one augur or priest of the mysteries could meet
another in the street "without laughing each other in
the face." Some of the most popular Grecian oracles
"owed their celebrity to exhilarating or stupefying
gases issuing from subterranean caverns." Yet the
wealth which they accumulated was, like the funds of
Odd-fellowship, fabulous. "One hundred and seven-
teen tiles of gold were presented to one of these oracles
by a single man. — Stowe'a Int., p. 177.

The philosophy of their power over mind is in the
fact that they are each a counterfeit salt'athn. The
prayer at the dedication of a cemetery asks the lodge
god to " puriiy our hearts, elevate our desires, and fit ua
for thy service." And the Grand Master aeaures the
Odd-fellows that over their " departed dwells ever the
bright halo of the hope of a glorious resurrection." —
Grosh, pp. 374,375.

And this Golden Rule Degree assures the brethren
that " Confessions of faith ' are mere " differences of
opinion." "Followers of different teachers, ye ore wor-
shipers of one God, who is Father of all, and therefore
ye are brethren."

That is, theirs is the Masonic reli^on, in which all
mankind agree : and every reflectiuEj child must
understand that the end of this religion la the god and
" prince of this world." Each new degree is hut a new
modification and statement of this religion.

H!<ih PriVs^— Patriarch, T welcome you to ihis 1
pie of our Order. Your progress hither may havJ
peared tedious, but we trust that the-lessons yon 1
gathered by the wny will prove profitable. All liufl
excellence is the reward of perseverance, toil, and f
ger, such as we have endeavored to picture to thej
agination in the mimic journey of life through wB
you have been conducted — a scene that has not 1
rehearsed for idle amusement, but to awaken ratlfl
meditation in a mind as mature as yours. The uiC
tainties of life are ever present to the understandiiifl
considerate men. Literally blindfold, and \
every side with danger and temptation, we struggle
through this earthly pilgrimage. With desires never
gratified, we are the subjects of endless toil and care, of
never-ceasing hope and never-ending disappointmeLt.
The false and flattering charms, which in the distance
Bo attract our admiration, disappear the moment they
are placed within our reach. Frail mortals that we are,
we know not What a day or an hour may bring forth.
Encompassed with peril on every side, with the seeds of
disease implanted in our nature, and the very air we
breathe impregnated with death, all the promises of life
are but dust. They fade as a leaf, and pass as the
shadow that fleeth away. How essential, then, that we
should understand our true position, and keep constant-
ly in view the realities that surround us 1 How essential
that we should learn to practice those living and im-
mortal virtues, which, while they secure ultimate hap-
piness, contribute so largely to smooth the troubles and
soften the asperities of life!

Tile secret orders have exhausted the vocabulary of
Ingcraft and priestcraft, which have scourged our race
ir 8ix thousand years and still scourge it. And, during
all that time, regalia, mock mysteries and sounding
titles have been their stock in trade and tools of incan-
tation.

This Royal Purple Degree ends the present sorcery
of Odd-fellowship, amid purple gowns, yellow belts,
black turbans and white surplices, with mitres and
lireaatplates. (See ritual, p. 303.) And ia this, as in
preceding degrees, " special " warning is given against
"rough usage" of the candidate, which shows the
'hole thing to be a religious horse-play. But one
ground idea underlies and runs through the whole. It


S96 A HIHIO JOUHKBT THIKHJ8H tlFB TO HBATEIT.

is a misletoe on the tree of life; a travesty and degrad-
ing caricature of God's system and Christ's way tv
heaven. The professed purpose and intent of this de-
gree, as given by the contrivers (ritual, p. 214), la " a
mimic journey of life."

Now the Bible picture of the Christian's life-jonmey
is to " run with patience," " through much tribulation."
" looking to Jesus," " by whom we have access " to the
infinite and eternal God.

Christ existed " before the foundation of the world,"
and has, ever since, been the " Way " for a man out of
the trouble and guilt of sin into the rest and holiness
of heaven. This crowning "royal purple degree"
mimics or burlesques this " way '■' in God's eternal sys-
tem of redemption. Thus: The candidate goes blind-
fold, looking on nothing, insteadl^f Christ. The " tribu-
lation" is " mimicked " by leading him over " stones.
pieces of wood, stumps, limbs of trees or brushwood."
(See ritual,, p. 203.) But thu guide of the poor simple-
ton is forbidden ■' all fast walking, rushing or rough
nsage of the candidate." This is the devil's mimicry of
Christ's "strait and tkornij road," through a world
which sin has made one of suffering.

And when the candidate has been badgered and bam-
boozled over the " stoues, brush, stumps," etc., in the
night, across a "bridge two feet wide, with rough poles
as railings," over a swollen stream, amid home-made
" thunder " and other frights, he is brought by the guide
into the Odd-fellows' mimic heaven, his ears tilled with
Bweet music, asid he is told that he is among " people
full of loy^r— Ritual, p. 313.

It is not a happen-so that, from the old Egyptian and
Greek Mysteries down through Freemasonry and 0<h

fellowship to little secret college clubs, this " mimicry "
of the struggle with sin and sorrow through to peace
with God is kept up. " Our rites," says Grosh, " begin
in gloom and end iu joy." A.ad in this he says Odd-
fellowship is a copy of all secret religious orders.

The students of Cornell University, N. Y., bound
young Mortimer D. Leggett's hands behind him, at
midnight, and rau him up and down a bluff six miles
out of Utica, N. Y.,and when wearied and bruised with
" rough usage," pulled off his blinder to make him, at
midnight, look down the bluff (by measure) forty feet,
where they had prepared an artificial hell, whose flames
were Chinese lanterns, and its devils students in horned
masques! They intended, after giving him a devifa
" broken heart." to lead him to " mimic " " joys of sal-
vation." m nightly revelry and debauch; but, being too
drunk to carry out the programme, they let him fall
down the bluff and broke his neck. And when the poor
boy was dying- -when the dimness of death was coming
over him^ — he ttought they were putting on the hood-
wink, and beggftd pit:oualy, "Don'tl Take it off! Do
take it off !" The coroner's inquest revealed these facts
to President White and his Faculty, but the literal mills
of hell still grind on in Cornell University, and others.
Sir.ilar, if not worse, " initiations " are practiced in
Tale College. A New Haven daily told the putlic that.
at one of these initiations into a Greek-letter fraterni-
ty, one student had his arm broken by " rough usage,"
and several others were su bruised that they had to be
taken home in a carriage after the savage handling and
night of debauch.

for sin " and " rest to the soul " in Christ all have re-

Tl 3 anci.-it masteries, too, like the modem, were all

And as every slice cut off from the Polyp becomes a
new animal, so of these mysteries, ancient and modern
alike; every offshoot, and even every new degree of an
offahoot, becomes a perfect secret order, putting the in-
itiate through from gloom to gaiety, from fear to fun
and frolic, from awe and terror to joy and jollification.
In short, the object is to produce in the initiate, aa
nearly as possible, feelings like those produced by the
Holy Spirit's correction of sin and joya of pardon, and
thus give him a devil's regeneration, in which Satan
officiates aa the Holy Ghost.

The oracle of Apollo at Delphi, according to Diodo-

the cavern, whereupon he also was seized with a like fit
F madness, leaping and dancing, and foretelling future
events."

only recompense you can hope for^ — the only praise to
which you can aspire. We have none other to offer.

great commandment — "Thou shall love thy neighbor as

It is difficult to analyze thia degree without indigKa-
tion and disgust. It is a degree which is not a degree,
but an excrescence. It inducts women into a secret or-
der, intended and named only for men (see p. 236), and
kept to its masculine gender from its origin to the jasx
of grace 1851. It is a woman's degree, practiced in the
sight, in which men, known and unknown to the initi-
ates, may be a majority of ten to one (see note, p. 23S),
(rbile women, as before, are excluded trom the Order it-
jelf; and, while the degree itself is an organized tempta-
tion, by its formal and flimsy guards against pollution
it suggests the verj' evil against which it affects i'i
piard.

With the customary formalities of an oath, with hov
right hand on the Bible fp. 244), the deluded woman is;
dedged to conceal — not, indeed, the secrets of Odd-fel-
Dwabip, of which she is permitted to know uothia^..

onliiute Lodges, " and "to indace them (members) to

, proRress upward in the Order"; i. t., pay for-more de-

8. " Leaima and ultimately destroy the prejudice felt

■gfunnt the Order by many of the fairer ses," which

** prevents accessions of members." (Pp. 333,234.) In

K word, we shall get more money hy it.

Thru summary of selfish, money-making motives, re-
member, is given by the inventor of the degree. They
were the motives by which he carried it. And when
e remember that ttie Grand Lodge statistics show that
ibe Order receives three dallarninduestoone paid back
a benefits — taking these avowed motives and this pub-
linhed practice together the Order must, on its own
authority, be regarded as a gigantic swindle. And
nothing but the wide reputation of Mr. Colfax, and
the Huccess and popularity of the imposition, pre-
vents its being handled by the law like mock-auctions,
lotteries, and other swindling practices. And the ben-
efits, fiiich as they are, being paid indiscriminately to

WOUAJS miDER FALSE BELIfllONS. 2S9

tnoD, Trould not justify a natioual appeal against it,
were not this secret order, like the others, a false relig-
ion, aa is shown abundantly in the Analyses of the Order
proper. Even the poor Rebekah-ite, after being be-
idrabbled through her weak decree, is told that she is
now " within our sacbed ciecle," and receives a pink
■nd green ribbon as an " emblem of eternal lote."{ !)
(Seep. 254.) The Bible is quoted abundantly to make
the farce solemn, and the most fulsome adulation of
woman, merely as a sex, is poured into the ears of the
initiate throughout the lecture of the degree. Who-
ever reads and ponders the degree itself will see that its
wicked and hateful nature is not and cannot be over-
stated.

The Philippian damsel who "brought her masters much
min by soothsaying" (Acts 16: 16), is a type of the
condition of women in all false religions, ancient and
modern.

A missionary of the A. B. C. F. M. informed the
writer that no females were educated by the pagans in
hia part of Asia but a few brilliant and beautiful girls
for the uses of the temple and companionship of the
priests. Two thousand such were kept in the single
Temple of Diana at Ephesus, which was 220 years in
bnilding, at the expense of all Asia Minor. The priests
were pimps and panderers to the prostitution of these
anfortunates, and gained untold wealth by renting their
persona. The poor Pythian damsel at Delphi was placed
npon a tripod over the exhilarating gaa, when " she
immediately began to swell and foam at the mouth,
tearing her hair and cutting her flesh." Her masters
took her ravings, while in this state, for oracles, and
interpreted them for money. Such are some of tha

To the Sovereign 6fand Lodge of the I/td^endent
Order ((f Odd J'ellotos .-

Your committee, to whom was referred the resolution
of Bep. Furley, of Kansas, relating to additional legisla-
tion for tJie government of Uniformed Degree Camps,
and also that changes be made in the secret working of
the degree, ao that the same may be made more effec-
tive and interesting to the candidate, beg leave to report
that a large number of memorials, petitions, and resolu-
tions, coming from very many juris diction a, have been
presented at this session and referred to your committee,
relating in various ways to legislation for the degree,
as well as for the government of the Camps themsplvea,
thereby showing an imperative demand for a degree and
the necessary legislation for the government and the
successful working of the various Camps instituted in
accordance therewith, that shall meet with the demands
and necessities of the fraternity at large.

The session of this Grand Ivodge is too short, and the
work committed to its committee ia too large, to enable
us to give BO important a matter the careful considera-
tJOQ and attention that it deserves, either at this or any
Other session.

We believe that, in order to make the degree what
it ehonld be, and to give itthat position and importance
by way of legislation for its government, which the large
membership demands, the whole subject-matter should
be referred to a committee of three, with instruoUons to
what the demands of the fraternity require, aa

THE PATRIARCHS MILITANT DEGREE.,

HiBTOBICAL AND InTKODUCTOBY.


This is a new degree in Odd-fellowsbip. It is an ii
portant event, at least to the five or six hundred tl
Band Odd-fellows; and to all thoughtful Americans who
concern tliemaelvea about the destiny of the United
States and that of the world as affected by our destiny,

I propose to consider: 1st. The History. £nd. Mo-
tives. Srd. Nature, and 4th. Relations of this degi'ee.

The history of Odd-fellowship as given in "Revised
Odd-fellowship Illustrated* Chapter I, (from Standard
writers; particularly from Qrosk) with the ritual, now
osed in many Odd-fellow lodges, and the accompanying
notes from their highest standard authorities put th^
whole system of Odd-fellowship; its origin, nature ;
history, under a shower of sunbeams.

The order thus (Revised Odd-fellowship pp. 21-30j
appears to have sprung up from old English olubs i
immemorial usage, such as still exist in the back Btreet|
and blind alleys of London. There amid beei'-n
dirty dealboard tables, the poorer class of mechai

and tlieir wives, even with babes at thoirbosatnai (El
